      18. Documentation Package
       • README - project overview and quick start.
       • ARCHITECTURE.md - system components and data flow.
       • SETUP.md - local development setup.
       • DEPLOYMENT.md - staging/production deployment.
       • API documentation / Postman collection.
       • Database schema and migration documentation.
       • ENVIRONMENT.md / .env.example.
       • ADMIN MANUAL - operational usage.
       • USER MANUAL where required.
       • HANDOVER.md - final ownership, services, URLs, repositories, known issues and transfer status.
       • CHANGELOG or release notes for final delivery.
       • Known limitations / deferred scope / third-party dependencies.



      19. Testing, Security & Quality Evidence
       • Unit/integration/end-to-end tests included where they exist or are required by scope.
       • UAT checklist and sign-off evidence.
       • Critical security checks: auth boundaries, permissions, input validation, secret handling, upload controls and
         dependency vulnerabilities.
       • No known critical build errors or unresolved production blockers at final sign-off unless explicitly accepted.
       • Production logs should not expose passwords, tokens or private secrets.
       • Backup and restore procedure tested where backend/database exists.
       • Final app/web build should correspond to the handed-over source commit.



      20. Fresh-Machine Handover Acceptance Test
       Mandatory practical        The strongest handover test is whether a developer who did not build the project can run it from the
       test                       handed-over package.



       1. Use a clean machine/user environment without the original developer's local files.
       2. Clone/download the client-owned repository.
       3. Follow README/SETUP instructions only.
       4. Install dependencies using the documented versions.
       5. Create environment files from .env.example and the secure credential register.
       6. Run database migrations/restore as documented.
       7. Start frontend/mobile/admin/backend services.
       8. Login and execute the agreed critical flows.
       9. Create a production build.
       10. Confirm another developer can identify the file/module for each major feature and continue editing it.

       Acceptance failure         If the project cannot run without the original vendor's machine/account/undocumented step, handover is
                                  incomplete until that dependency is removed or formally accepted.

      21. IP / Ownership / Vendor-Lock-In Clause
      Suggested contract wording (subject to final legal/commercial review):
      Upon receipt of the payments required under the governing agreement, the Vendor shall hand over to the Client the complete
      editable source code and project materials created specifically for the Project, including frontend, backend, mobile, admin,
      database migrations/schema, configuration templates, technical documentation, design/source assets included in scope,
      deployment instructions and transferable account/project access. The Vendor shall not intentionally retain any undisclosed
      technical dependency that prevents the Client or a replacement developer from operating, maintaining, modifying or deploying
      the Project. Third-party and open-source components remain subject to their respective licences. Production secrets shall be
      transferred securely and not embedded in public source code.

      Use this paragraph in every new proposal/SRD/SOW:
      "Attachment A - Source Code Modularity & Final Handover Requirements is incorporated into this Project scope. The
      Vendor shall structure the software in a maintainable page-wise/feature-wise/module-wise manner appropriate to the
      selected technology and shall deliver the complete editable project and handover package described in Attachment A.
      Any conflict with a project-specific SRD/SOW should be resolved in writing before development proceeds."

       UAT / Acceptance
       Representative




       Final principle            The goal is not merely to receive "code". The goal is to receive a complete, modular, reproducible,
                                  documented and transferable software project that another developer can safely continue.
